P0096: continuous-law counterexample to the four-edge lower bound

Status update19:18Z2026-09-06: ALREADY REFUTED IN THE LITERATURE. Ahlberg1911.09949 (2019;published2020) explicitly settles this question. The proof below is retained as an author-checked rederivation; no novelty or original solved-open credit. The two-page primary paper was read in full; no independent review of this document occurred.

Source and exact target

Auffinger, Damron and Hanson, *50 years of first-passage percolation*, arXiv:1511.03262, manuscript26September2016, Question12 (PDF p.33; repeated p.146), asks whether iid nonnegative edge passage times on Z^2 with F(0)=0 always satisfy

\[\mu(e_1)\geq \mathbb E\min(\tau_1,\tau_2,\tau_3,\tau_4).\]

E_4 denotes expectation of a minimum of four independent copies, not a fourth moment. The survey motivates this by Alm-Deijfen's simulation study, arXiv:1412.5924 (December2014). That paper describes an observed relation for selected distribution families. The target here is the survey's universal formulation, not a universal theorem attributed to the simulation authors.

Primary sources: https://arxiv.org/pdf/1511.03262 and https://arxiv.org/pdf/1412.5924. The two standard time-constant inputs below are stated in the latter paper, equations(2),(3), PDF p.3.

Construction and lower bound

Independently for each lattice edge e, take eta_e in {0,1} with P(eta_e=0)=3/4 and an independent U_e uniform on [0,1]. For 0<epsilon<=1 put

\[\tau_e^{(\epsilon)}=\eta_e+\epsilon U_e.\]

The resulting iid law is bounded and absolutely continuous: its density is 3/(4epsilon) on (0,epsilon) and 1/(4epsilon) on (1,1+epsilon). It has no atoms, F_epsilon(0)=0, and infimum of support zero. Every moment exists.

For four independent copies, the event that all eta values equal1 has probability (1/4)^4=1/256. Their minimum is at least1 on this event, so

\[\mathbb E\min_{1\le i\le4}\tau_i^{(\epsilon)}\geq1/256.\tag{1}\]

Time constants tend to zero

For bounded iid nonnegative edge weights the established subadditive formula is

\[\mu(e_1)=\inf_{n\ge1}\mathbb E T(0,ne_1)/n.\tag{2}\]

The established positivity criterion says mu(e_1)>0 iff F(0)<p_c(Z^2)=1/2. Hence the eta-only model has mu_0=0 because its zero mass is3/4. Each positive-epsilon model has mu_epsilon>0.

Fix n and couple all epsilon values with the same eta,U. Trivially T_epsilon(0,ne_1)>=T_0(0,ne_1). Given delta>0, choose a finite path gamma whose eta-cost is at most T_0(0,ne_1)+delta. Such a path exists by the definition of an infimum; a minimizing path is not assumed. Since U_e<=1,

\[T_\epsilon(0,ne_1)\le T_0(0,ne_1)+\delta+\epsilon|\gamma|.\]

First send epsilon to zero and then delta to zero. This proves T_epsilon(0,ne_1) tends almost surely to T_0(0,ne_1). The straight coordinate path gives 0<=T_epsilon(0,ne_1)<=2n. Dominated convergence therefore gives convergence of expectations.

For each fixed n, (2) now implies

\[\limsup_{\epsilon\downarrow0}\mu_\epsilon(e_1)
\leq \mathbb E T_0(0,ne_1)/n.\]

Take the infimum over n and use mu_0=0. Nonnegativity yields

\[\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow0}\mu_\epsilon(e_1)=0.\tag{3}\]

By(1),(3), every sufficiently small positive epsilon satisfies

\[0<\mu_\epsilon(e_1)<1/256\leq\mathbb E\min_{i\le4}\tau_i^{(\epsilon)}.\]

Thus this explicit family eventually reverses the proposed universal inequality. The proof gives existence of a small-enough parameter, not a numerical cutoff. All sufficiently large integers k give examples with epsilon=1/k.

Adversarial checks and limits

The argument never interchanges an infinite-path minimization with a limit. It uses a finite approximate minimizer for each fixed n, then an upper bound valid for every n. No uniform bound on geodesic length is needed.

The limiting distribution has an atom at zero; the actual counterexample distributions do not. Their support has a gap, which the survey's stated hypotheses do not prohibit. Thus this is stronger than an atomic-law loophole.

A local entrance cost remains positive even when long travel can exploit a supercritical cheap-edge network; dividing a single entrance cost by distance explains the failure of the proposed bound. As a second literature lane, Cox-Kesten continuity of the time constant, cited in Alm-Deijfen, gives the same limiting conclusion for this bounded weakly convergent family. The direct proof above does not need that general continuity theorem.

Searches for Question12, Alm-Deijfen lower bounds, and minimum-of-four counterexamples did not locate a matching prior construction in the inspected results. This does not establish novelty. Before promotion, recheck source notation and standard theorem hypotheses, compare the original simulation discussion and later errata, and obtain independent review. No mathematical gap has been identified in the current argument, but neither independent review nor current open status is claimed.

Later-literature correction and an explicit parameter

Ahlberg, Tertiles and the time constant, https://arxiv.org/pdf/1911.09949, Theorem1 and final paragraph, already gives counterexamples. The publication is Journal of Applied Probability57(2),407-408(2020), DOI10.1017/jpr.2020.9. The earlier search statement above records the preliminary search only and is superseded by this finding.

For our family, its two-thirds quantile is8epsilon/9. Ahlberg proves mu<=2t_(2/3), so epsilon=1/1024 gives mu<=1/576<1/256<=E minfour. This is an explicit atomless bounded example derived from an established theorem. The continuity argument above remains a separate qualitative derivation. No original solution is claimed.

Proof appendix: P0140_thinned_levy_random_drift_candidate

Candidate and author-check labels inside this appendix govern its status. Mathematical source notation is preserved.

P0140: Poisson marking explains the random critical drift

Status: author-checked derivation, no independent review, no novelty claim. Excluded from the current-open target and original-solution counts. The filename preserves the initial candidate workflow. The later primary paper already states an indirect identification with Joseph's independent-increment limit. What is supplied here is a direct calculation and an explicit obstruction to holding the critical drift deterministic.

Sources: van der Hofstad, *Stochastic processes on random graphs*, July12,2017, equations4.5.5 and4.5.20, Open Problem4.1, PDF pp.206,210; https://www.win.tue.nl/~rhofstad/SaintFlour_SPoRG_fin_Saint_Flour.pdf . Dhara,van der Hofstad,van Leeuwaarden,Sen, *Heavy-tailed configuration models at criticality*, December2019 version, equation2.22 and Remark11, PDF pp.9-10; https://arxiv.org/pdf/1612.00650 . Joseph's original independent-increment limit: https://arxiv.org/abs/1012.2352 . Exact comparison with every convention in Joseph's characteristic function remains a separate check; the independence statement below is direct.

Setup and the distinction in the question

Fix 3<tau<4, c>0, and mu>0. Let Gamma_i be the arrival times of a rate-one Poisson process and set theta_i=(c/Gamma_i)^(1/(tau-1)). Thus the theta_i form a Poisson point process on(0,infinity) with intensity

rho(dx)=(tau-1)c x^(-tau) dx.

Conditionally on these points, give each theta_i an independent exponential clock T_i with rate theta_i/mu. The source's process with a deterministic real parameter lambda is

S_lambda(t)=sum_i theta_i[1{T_i<=t}-(theta_i/mu)t]+lambda*t.

The ordered series is well defined. For small theta_i, its conditional means are O(theta_i^3) and its conditional variances are O(theta_i^3); sum theta_i^3 is finite almost surely. There are finitely many points above1, each finite. A process-level construction also follows from the compensated Poisson representation below.

The important parameter for iid degrees is the RANDOM variable Lambda_0 in equation2.22 of the later paper, not a fixed lambda. Define

Z=lim_(epsilon down0)[sum_(theta_i>epsilon) theta_i^2 - integral_(epsilon,infinity) x^2 rho(dx)].

The small-point part of this compensated Poisson integral converges in L2 and almost surely because integral_(0,1) x^4 rho(dx)<infinity. The large-point part is finite almost surely and has finite first absolute moment because integral_(1,infinity) x^2 rho(dx)<infinity. Its second moment need not be finite. Set Lambda_0=Z/mu.

At the random cutoff epsilon=theta_n, including the nth point changes the sum only by theta_n^2, which tends to0. Since

integral_(theta_n,infinity) x^2 rho(dx)=c^(2/(tau-1))*Gamma_n^(1-2/(tau-1))/(1-2/(tau-1)),

the definition of Z/mu is exactly equation2.22 after telescoping the integrals over[Gamma_(i-1),Gamma_i]. This checks the random-drift convention against the primary source, not merely by analogy.

Direct independent-increment representation

For every epsilon>0 the cancellation is an identity:

S_0,epsilon(t)+(t/mu)Z_epsilon
= sum_(theta_i>epsilon) theta_i 1{T_i<=t} -(t/mu) integral_(epsilon,infinity) x^2 rho(dx).

By independent Poisson marking, the pairs(theta_i,T_i) form a Poisson random measure with deterministic intensity

nu(dx,ds)=rho(dx)*(x/mu)*exp(-xs/mu) ds.

The jump sum in the last display has independent increments in t, since disjoint time intervals select disjoint sets of this Poisson measure. Its centering is deterministic. Passing to finite-dimensional limits preserves independence, so

L(t):=S_0(t)+(t/mu)Z=S_(Lambda_0)(t)

has independent increments. They are generally NONSTATIONARY: the jump intensity contains exp(-xs/mu).

For completeness, this passage is not based on a formally divergent cancellation. Write the truncated expression as M_epsilon(t)+D_epsilon(t), where

M_epsilon(t)=sum_(theta_i>epsilon) theta_i1{T_i<=t}
             -integral_(epsilon,infinity) x(1-exp(-xt/mu))rho(dx),

D_epsilon(t)=integral_(epsilon,infinity) x[1-exp(-xt/mu)-xt/mu]rho(dx).

On bounded time intervals, the centered contribution from points x<=1 is an L2 martingale in t. The variance contributed by0<x<=epsilon is bounded by

(T/mu) integral_(0,epsilon) x^3 rho(dx),

which tends to0 because tau<4. Doob's inequality gives uniform-in-time L2 convergence of this small-point martingale. The deterministic drift converges uniformly on bounded time intervals because its integrand near0 is O(x^3), uniformly for bounded t. The finitely many large points and their finite deterministic compensator cause no problem. Hence L has a cadlag version obtained by this construction, and S_0=L-(t/mu)Z does too.

The deterministic drift can also be evaluated:

D(t)= - (tau-1)c * Gamma(4-tau)/[(tau-2)(tau-3)] * (t/mu)^(tau-2).

To check the sign and constant, substitute u=xt/mu and integrate twice by parts in the positive integral of u^(1-tau)(exp(-u)-1+u). All boundary terms vanish for3<tau<4. The remaining integral is Gamma(4-tau)/[(tau-2)(tau-3)]. In particular D(t)<0, consistent with depletion of previously discovered vertices.

A deterministic lambda does not have independent increments

This part checks the strongest way the preceding conclusion could be misreported. Consider increments A=S_0(mu) and B=S_0(2mu)-S_0(mu). A marked point of size x contributes

F1=x1{T<=mu}-x^2,
F2=x1{mu<T<=2mu}-x^2.

Let K(a,b)=log E exp(aA+bB), for a,b>=0. These Laplace transforms are finite. Indeed, large points have nonpositive F1,F2 once x>=1; for small points the linear terms have integrable expectation O(x^3) and the quadratic remainder has integrable expectation O(x^3). The compensated Poisson formula, or its finite-cutoff version followed by these bounds, gives

K(a,a)-K(a,0)-K(0,a)
=integral rho(dx) E_T[(exp(aF1)-1)(exp(aF2)-1)].

For x>=1 both F1 and F2 are nonpositive, so the integrand is nonnegative. If0<a<=1/4 and x lies in[a^(-1/2),2a^(-1/2)], then x>=2 and F1,F2<=-x^2/2. Consequently the integrand is at least(1-exp(-1/2))^2 on this interval. Its rho-mass is

c*(1-2^(1-tau))*a^((tau-1)/2).

The potentially negative part is confined to0<x<1. The two indicators in F1,F2 are disjoint, and

E|F1F2| <= x^3 P(T<=2mu)+x^4 <=3x^4.

Since |exp(au)-1|<=a|u|exp(a|u|) and |Fj|<=2 on this range, the absolute contribution of this whole region is at most

3a^2 exp(4a) integral_(0,1) x^4 rho(dx)=C*a^2 exp(4a).

Thus the mixed log-Laplace difference is bounded below by a positive constant times a^((tau-1)/2) minus C*a^2 exp(4a). Since (tau-1)/2 is strictly less than2, it is positive for all sufficiently small a>0. Independence would make this difference identically0. Therefore A and B are dependent. Adding any deterministic lambda changes only their deterministic means and cannot fix this dependence.

This is a direct counterexample to interpreting the 2017 question as a claim about deterministic lambda after mixing the weights. It does not refute the corrected iid-degree statement, whose random drift is explicitly supplied in the later paper.

Checks and remaining status limits

The argument has two complementary checks: Poisson marking proves independence after the exact random-slope cancellation; the mixed Laplace estimate disproves independence without it. The latter avoids covariance calculations, which would be invalid because the random slope can have infinite variance. The PPP intensity follows independently from the tail count E number{theta_i>x}=c*x^(-(tau-1)). The source's Gamma-coordinate expression telescopes to exactly the same compensated slope.

No simulation is used as proof. No independent referee has checked this text. The 2019 paper already identifies the corrected law indirectly, so this derivation does not establish an unproved theorem for the corpus and is not counted as an original solution. Later literature may also contain this direct marking calculation; that novelty search remains incomplete.

Proof appendix: P0174_free_brownian_unimodality_constant_candidate

Candidate and author-check labels inside this appendix govern its status. Mathematical source notation is preserved.

P0174: candidate sharp diameter threshold for free Brownian unimodality

Status: complete candidate proof, author-checked in two analytic formulations; no independent review and no claim of established novelty. Current-literature search is incomplete. Do not count as a promoted original solution.

Source: Hasebe and Ueda, *Large time unimodality for classical and free Brownian motions with initial distributions*, ALEA 15 (2018), 353-374, Problem 3.3; archived arXiv1710.08240v2, PDF p.6. The PDF carries the arXiv revision date14 September2018 and a manuscript header4 November2021; retain that discrepancy. Primary links: https://arxiv.org/abs/1710.08240 and https://alea.math.cnrs.fr/articles/v15/15-15.pdf . The source defines S(0,t) to have variance t, and D_mu to be support diameter.

The source proves the sufficient constant4 and asks for the optimal universal C in t>=C D_mu^2. Its paragraph says the symmetric Bernoulli law on{-1,1} has threshold t=4 and diameter2, then prints2<=C<=4. Those preceding numbers imply the lower bound C>=1. The candidate upper bound below is a separate argument, not a conclusion drawn from that arithmetic inconsistency.

Candidate theorem

If mu is a compactly supported probability measure on R with support diameter D, then mu free-convolved with the centered semicircle law of variance t is unimodal whenever t>=D^2. The optimal universal constant in the source's normalization is therefore C=1.

When D=0 the measure is a point mass and the assertion is immediate. Henceforth D>0. No symmetry, density, or non-atomicity of mu is assumed.

An elementary moment inequality

Let Q be a probability law of a centered real variable X supported in an interval of length D. Write a=E_Q X^2 and b=E_Q X^4. Then

\[
 a\le D^2/4,\qquad b+3a^2\le D^2a.
\]

For the first inequality, if the supporting interval is[l,h], then E[(X-l)(h-X)]>=0 gives a<=-lh, and -lh<=D^2/4 because l+h has a real square. For the second, let X' be an independent copy. Pointwise, (X-X')^4<=D^2(X-X')^2. Taking expectations and using E X=0 gives2b+6a^2<=2D^2a. This derivation checks both the coefficient3 and the use of the diameter rather than radius.

Equality a=D^2/4 forces l=-D/2,h=D/2 and Q to put equal mass on the two endpoints: equality in E[(X-l)(h-X)]>=0 forces endpoint support, and centering fixes the weights.

Cauchy-profile lemma

For R>0 define

\[
 F_R(u)=\int\frac{d\mu(x)}{(x-u)^2+R^2}.
\]

Claim: F_R has no local minimum with value at most1/D^2.

Take any stationary point u of F_R with F_R''(u)>=0. Put y=R^2>0 and

\[
 Z=\int ((x-u)^2+y)^{-2}\,d\mu(x),\qquad
 dQ(x)=Z^{-1}((x-u)^2+y)^{-2}\,d\mu(x).
\]

Differentiation under the integral is justified by R>0 and compact support. Stationarity says E_Q(X-u)=0. With a=E_Q(X-u)^2 and b=E_Q(X-u)^4, the preceding moment inequalities apply. The second derivative is

\[
 \frac{F_R''(u)}{2Z}
 =E_Q\frac{3(X-u)^2-y}{(X-u)^2+y}
 =3-4y E_Q\frac1{(X-u)^2+y}.
\]

Jensen's inequality and F_R''(u)>=0 imply

\[
 \frac1{a+y}\le E_Q\frac1{(X-u)^2+y}\le\frac3{4y},
 \qquad y\le3a.
\]

The normalization of Q gives an exact reciprocal-profile identity:

\[
 \frac1{F_R(u)}
 =\frac{E_Q[((X-u)^2+y)^2]}{E_Q[(X-u)^2+y]}
 =\frac{b+2ay+y^2}{a+y}.
\]

Consequently

\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \frac1{F_R(u)}-D^2
 &\le\frac{y^2+2ay-3a^2-D^2y}{a+y}\\
 &=\frac{(y-3a)(y+a)+(4a-D^2)y}{a+y}\le0.
 \end{aligned}
\]

The first inequality uses b<=D^2a-3a^2; the final two summands are nonpositive because y<=3a and4a<=D^2. In particular, F_R(u)>=1/D^2.

The equality case matters for the endpoint t=D^2. If F_R(u)=1/D^2 and F_R''(u)>=0, then the two final nonpositive terms both vanish: y=3a and4a=D^2, since y>0. Equality in the variance bound forces Q to be symmetric Bernoulli on u plus/minus D/2. On these two points the reweighting denominator is equal, so mu is the same symmetric Bernoulli law. Writing c=D/2 and s for displacement from its midpoint, with R^2=3c^2, gives

\[
 F_R(u+s)=\frac{s^2+4c^2}{s^4+4c^2s^2+16c^4},
 \qquad
 F_R(u+s)-\frac1{4c^2}
 =-\frac{s^4}{4c^2(s^4+4c^2s^2+16c^4)}<0\quad(s\ne0).
\]

Thus the only possible equality point is a strict maximum, not a local minimum. Every local minimum therefore has value strictly greater than1/D^2, proving the claim including equality.

Unimodality via Biane's density parametrization

For fixed t>=D^2 and fixed R>0, the equation F_R(u)=1/t has at most two solutions. Indeed, if there were three solutions u1<u2<u3, then either F_R dips below1/t somewhere in[u1,u3], producing an interior local minimum below that level, or F_R>=1/t throughout that interval, making u2 a local minimum at that level. Both alternatives contradict the Cauchy-profile lemma because1/t<=1/D^2. The analytic function cannot be identically constant on an interval, since F_R tends to0 at infinity.

Now use exactly the density framework stated in source Section2.2 and Lemma3.1. Define v_t(u)>=0 by

\[
 \int\frac{d\mu(x)}{(x-u)^2+v_t(u)^2}=\frac1t
\]

where its positive solution exists, setting v_t=0 elsewhere. Biane's real homeomorphism psi_t satisfies

\[
 p_t(\psi_t(u))=\frac{v_t(u)}{\pi t},
\]

where p_t is the continuous density of mu free-convolved with S(0,t), analytic wherever positive. Each positive density level p_t=a corresponds to F_(pi*a*t)(u)=1/t, and therefore has at most two points. Source Lemma2.2, equivalently Lemma3.1, now gives unimodality. All source assumptions match: mu is compactly supported, t>0, and the semicircle parameter is variance.

Sharpness

Let mu be symmetric Bernoulli on{-c,c}, c>0, so D=2c. For c^2<t<4c^2, the positive solution at the symmetry center has v_t(0)^2=t-c^2. Implicit differentiation at0 yields

\[
 v_t''(0)=\frac{3c^2-v_t(0)^2}{v_t(0)(c^2+v_t(0)^2)}
 =\frac{4c^2-t}{t\sqrt{t-c^2}}>0.
\]

Thus v_t has a positive strict local minimum at0, and the density under the homeomorphism psi_t does as well. It is not unimodal. For any proposed C<1, choose t strictly between max(C,1/4)D^2 and D^2. Then t>=CD^2 but unimodality fails. This proves the lower bound C>=1 independently of the source's printed inequality.

Second analytic check and remaining review obligations

The same moment computation can be applied directly at critical points of v_t. At such a point, the profile's first derivative vanishes and the sign of v_t'' equals the sign of F_v-fixed''. The calculation above says a nonnegative second derivative would force t<=D^2. Hence for t>D^2 every critical point of v_t is a strict maximum, giving the same threshold. The equality analysis in the Cauchy-profile proof closes the boundary without appealing to weak closure of unimodal laws.

The load-bearing points to challenge are the Q-normalization identity, the centered fourth-moment inequality, the direction of Jensen, and the equality case at t=D^2. Each is written explicitly; neither a numerical validator nor brain retrieval supplies independent mathematical review. Later literature might already contain this sharp threshold or the same argument. The statement remains a candidate until further proof checking and literature comparison are complete.

Proof appendix: P0189_brown_measure_strong_convergence_counterexample_candidate

Candidate and author-check labels inside this appendix govern its status. Mathematical source notation is preserved.

P0189: candidate counterexample to Brown-measure continuity under strong convergence

Status: complete candidate construction, checked by the author; no independent review and no novelty claim. This addresses the literal general conjecture in van Handel, *The strong convergence phenomenon*, arXiv2507.00346v2 (11 February2026), Section6.9, pp.69-70. It does not address the narrower conjectures for independent Ginibre matrices or free limiting families.

Primary source for the question: https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.00346 . Primary source for the established limiting Brown measure: Fang, Jiang, Lin and Xu, *On generalized universal irrational rotation algebras and the operator u+v*, https://arxiv.org/abs/1210.4771 , Theorem12.2. Its introduction also states simplicity and uniqueness of trace of the irrational rotation algebra. These literature theorems are dependencies, not results proved here.

Finite matrices

Fix an irrational theta in(0,1). Choose reduced rational approximants p_j/q_j tending to theta, with q_j tending to infinity. Let omega_j=exp(2*pi*i*p_j/q_j). On the standard basis indexed by k modulo q_j define

\[
 U_j e_k=\omega_j^k e_k,\qquad
 S_j e_k=e_{k-1},\qquad
 V_j=e^{\pi i/q_j}S_j.
\]

Then U_j and V_j are unitary and satisfy

\[
 V_jU_j=\omega_jU_jV_j,\qquad U_j^{q_j}=I,\qquad V_j^{q_j}=-I.
\]

Put A_j=U_j+V_j. Its characteristic polynomial is exactly z^{q_j}. Indeed, in the determinant of zI-U_j-V_j only the all-diagonal permutation and the full cyclic permutation contribute (for q_j>=2). The former contributes

\[
 \prod_{k=0}^{q_j-1}(z-\omega_j^k)=z^{q_j}-1,
\]

because omega_j is primitive. The latter contributes minus the product of the cyclic weights, namely -exp(pi*i)=+1. Thus det(zI-A_j)=z^{q_j}. Cayley-Hamilton gives A_j^{q_j}=0, and every empirical eigenvalue distribution is delta_0.

An equivalent algebraic check is the root-of-unity binomial identity (U_j+V_j)^{q_j}=U_j^{q_j}+V_j^{q_j}=0. The determinant calculation supplies the conclusion without relying on that identity.

Strong convergence of the pair

Let A_theta be the universal unital irrational rotation C*-algebra generated by unitaries u,v satisfying vu=exp(2*pi*i*theta)uv. This algebra is simple and has a unique faithful tracial state tau, as recalled in the cited primary paper.

Consider the norm quotient

\[
 \mathcal Q=\prod_j M_{q_j}(\mathbb C)\big/\bigoplus_j M_{q_j}(\mathbb C),
\]

where the direct sum consists of sequences whose operator norms tend to zero. The classes of (U_j) and (V_j) are unitaries and satisfy the irrational relation exactly, since omega_j tends to exp(2*pi*i*theta). Universality therefore gives a unital *-homomorphism A_theta to Q. Simplicity makes it injective, hence isometric. Since the quotient norm of a bounded sequence is its limsup norm, for every noncommutative *-polynomial P,

\[
 \limsup_j\|P(U_j,V_j,U_j^*,V_j^*)\|
 =\|P(u,v,u^*,v^*)\|.
\]

The same argument applies to every subsequence. If the norms failed to converge to the displayed value, a subsequence uniformly separated from that value would contradict the same limsup identity. Consequently the pair converges strongly.

The normalized traces also converge to tau. Using the commutation relation, every fixed word is a phase times U_j^a V_j^b. When b is a fixed nonzero integer and j is large, the shift has no diagonal entries, so its trace is zero. If b=0 and a is fixed nonzero, the geometric sum of omega_j^{ak} is zero for all q_j>|a|. The identity word has trace one, and phases converge. These are exactly the canonical irrational-rotation trace values. Thus both norm and tracial versions of strong convergence are satisfied, with a faithful limiting trace.

In particular A_j converges strongly, including all *-polynomials in A_j alone, to a=u+v.

The survey Definition 1.1 also allows fixed finite-dimensional matrix coefficients. For each fixed D, amplify the quotient homomorphism to M_D(A_theta). An injective *-homomorphism is completely isometric, and M_D(Q) identifies with the quotient of the product of M_D(M_qj) by norm-null sequences. Therefore the same limsup identity and subsequence argument apply to every D-by-D matrix-valued *-polynomial. The identification follows entrywise in finite D: a matrix sequence tends to zero in norm exactly when all its entries do, and the quotient norm is the limsup of the block-matrix norms. Thus the precise matrix-coefficient definition is covered.

A separate norm-convergence proof uses the trace computation above for the lower bound and needs no appeal to simplicity. Write P_j for a fixed matrix-valued polynomial and P for its limit. For every positive integer m, with normalized traces on the matrix factors,
\[
 \|P_j\|\ge\big[(\mathrm{tr}_D\otimes\mathrm{tr}_{q_j})((P_j^*P_j)^m)\big]^{1/(2m)}.
\]
Taking the lower limit in j and then m to infinity gives liminf ||P_j|| >= ||P||, because the limiting trace tr_D tensor tau is faithful. Complete contractivity of the universal quotient homomorphism gives limsup ||P_j|| <= ||P||. This independently proves the required full norm convergence. The parent suggested these two explicit extensions during its first bounded review on 6 September 2026; the author checked and incorporated them. The remaining review and novelty status is pending.

The Brown-measure mismatch

The cited Theorem12.2 states that the Brown measure of u+v, in the tracial von Neumann algebra generated by the irrational rotation pair, is Haar probability measure on the unit circle. It is therefore different from delta_0. The finite empirical measures are all delta_0 and do not converge to the Brown measure of a, despite strong convergence.

The same example also prevents a universal lower spectral-radius conclusion: every A_j has spectral radius zero, whereas the source Theorem11.1 gives spectrum of u+v equal to the closed unit disk and hence spectral radius one.

To make the matrices random rather than deterministic, take Haar unitary Q_j and replace the pair by (Q_j U_j Q_j*,Q_j V_j Q_j*). For every outcome, norms, normalized traces, nilpotency, and eigenvalue distributions are unchanged. This supplies genuinely random families satisfying the same convergence. The two members are dependent; the construction does not claim otherwise. Equivalently, the single random matrix Q_j A_j Q_j* is already a counterexample for a one-variable polynomial.

Optional realization in every integer dimension

The dimensions q_j suffice for a counterexample along a sequence tending to infinity. If an all-integer-dimension formulation is required, take consecutive continued-fraction denominators, which are coprime. For each sufficiently large N, choose the largest j with q_j*q_{j+1}<=N. The elementary coin theorem gives N=a*q_j+b*q_{j+1} for nonnegative integers a,b. Define the N-dimensional pair as the block direct sum of a copies of the j-th pair and b copies of the(j+1)-st pair. Both block dimensions tend to infinity as N does. Polynomial norms are the maximum of their block norms, traces are convex combinations of block traces, and the sum remains nilpotent. Thus the same counterexample exists with dimension exactly N for every sufficiently large integer N.

Scope and remaining checks

This is a candidate refutation of the survey's literal implication from arbitrary strong convergence to empirical Brown-measure convergence. It is not a refutation for independent-entry ensembles, free limits, or holomorphic polynomials in independent Ginibre matrices. Yi Han's June2026 paper, arXiv2606.01664, claims the latter case and is compatible with this construction.

Load-bearing obligations are the precise survey scope, the primitive-root determinant sign, isometry of the quotient homomorphism, faithful-trace interpretation, and the cited Brown-measure theorem. All are explicit here. It remains possible that the construction or the general failure of continuity is already known; current-literature novelty is unverified. No validator, numerical eigenvalue computation, or brain ingestion is treated as proof review. In fact numerical eigenvalues of these nilpotent matrices would be an especially unreliable check because of their extreme nonnormality.

Proof appendix: P0193_compact_multiplicative_nonunimodality_candidate

Candidate and author-check labels inside this appendix govern its status. Mathematical source notation is preserved.

P0193: a compactly supported initial law with persistent failure of log-unimodality

Status: complete author-checked candidate counterexample, 2026-09-06. No independent review and no novelty certification. This is a proposed negative answer to Hasebe, Ueda and Wang, *Log-unimodality for free positive multiplicative Brownian motion*, arXiv:2009.13848v1 (29 September 2020), Problem 4.7, PDF page 11. The published reference is Colloquium Mathematicum 169 (2022), 209-226; the archived version used here is the arXiv version. Source: https://arxiv.org/abs/2009.13848.

Claim and normalization

Let \(\nu=(\delta_{1/10}+\delta_{10})/2\). In the source normalization
\[\Sigma_{\sigma_t}(z)=\exp\!\left(\frac t2\frac{z+1}{z-1}\right),\]
the law \(\sigma_t\boxtimes\nu\) is **not log-unimodal for every \(t\ge5\pi\)**. Its initial law has support in the compact interval \([1/10,10]\), so this disproves eventual log-unimodality for arbitrary compactly supported positive initial laws, if the argument and source match survive review.

Log-unimodality means unimodality of the image measure under the logarithm; ordinary unimodality on the positive line is a different claim. The initial law is multiplicatively symmetric but is not log-unimodal. Thus the source theorem for symmetric log-unimodal initial laws does not apply.

The exact source criterion

Lemma 4.1(3), PDF page 8, says that \(\sigma_t\boxtimes\nu\) is log-unimodal if and only if, for every \(R\in(0,\pi)\), the equation
\[\frac{\sin R}{R}\int_0^\infty\frac{r\xi}{1+r^2\xi^2-2r\xi\cos R}\,d\nu(\xi)=\frac1t\tag{1}\]
has at most two solutions \(r>0\). We use its contrapositive by finding four distinct solutions for one value of \(R\), for each specified \(t\).

Put \(a=\log10\), \(u=\log r\), and
\[K_R(s)=\frac{1}{2(\cosh s-\cos R)},\qquad F_R(u)=\frac{K_R(u-a)+K_R(u+a)}2.\]
The integral in (1) equals \(F_R(u)\), because dividing its integrand's numerator and denominator by \(r\xi\) gives
\[\frac{r\xi}{1+r^2\xi^2-2r\xi\cos R}=\frac1{r\xi+(r\xi)^{-1}-2\cos R}=K_R(\log r+\log\xi).\]
Hence (1) is equivalent to
\[F_R(u)=\frac{R}{t\sin R}.\tag{2}\]
For fixed \(R\in(0,\pi)\), the function \(F_R\) is continuous, positive and even, and tends to zero at both infinities.

Selecting the level and time

The function \(g(R)=R/\sin R\) is strictly increasing from 1 to infinity on \((0,\pi)\). Indeed
\[g'(R)=\frac{\sin R-R\cos R}{\sin^2R}>0,\]
because the numerator vanishes at zero and has derivative \(R\sin R>0\). Also \(g(\pi/2)=\pi/2\).

For each \(t\ge5\pi\), there is therefore a unique \(R_t\in[\pi/2,\pi)\) with
\[g(R_t)=\frac t{10}.\]
For this choice the right side of (2) is exactly \(1/10\), and \(\cos R_t\le0\).

Four strict crossings

Since \(\cosh(\log10)=101/20\),
\[F_{R_t}(0)=K_{R_t}(a)=\frac1{2(101/20-\cos R_t)}\le\frac{10}{101}<\frac1{10}.\tag{3}\]
At \(u=a\), the contribution from the centered kernel alone gives
\[F_{R_t}(a)=\frac{K_{R_t}(0)+K_{R_t}(2a)}2\ge\frac1{4(1-\cos R_t)}\ge\frac18>\frac1{10}.\tag{4}\]
The same inequality holds at \(-a\) by evenness. Together with the limits zero at both infinities, (3)-(4) and the intermediate value theorem give a solution of \(F_{R_t}(u)=1/10\) in each of the four disjoint intervals
\[(-\infty,-a),\quad(-a,0),\quad(0,a),\quad(a,\infty).\]
Exponentiation is injective, so these give four distinct positive solutions of (1). Lemma 4.1 proves the claim.

Separate algebra check in the original variable

The same signs can be checked without hyperbolic functions. At \(r=1\), both atom integrands equal
\[\frac{10}{101-20\cos R}\le\frac{10}{101}<\frac1{10}.\]
At \(r=10\), the atom \(\xi=1/10\) contributes, including its mass one half,
\[\frac1{2(2-2\cos R)}=\frac1{4(1-\cos R)}\ge\frac18.\]
At \(r=1/10\), the atom \(\xi=10\) gives the same lower bound. The integral tends to zero as \(r\downarrow0\) and as \(r\uparrow\infty\). Thus there are crossings in \((0,1/10),(1/10,1),(1,10),(10,\infty)\). These strict rational inequalities include the boundary time \(5\pi\), for which \(R_t=\pi/2\).

Adversarial checks and limits

The crucial direction is the equivalence in Lemma 4.1, rather than a merely sufficient unimodality condition. Its full statement and proof must remain pinned to the source. The four crossings concern a positive density level, not just multiple extrema in an auxiliary transform. The time is selected separately for each \(t\), as the criterion permits. The upper bound on the central value is uniform for \(R\ge\pi/2\); the lower bound at the two flanks is uniform for all \(R\in(0,\pi)\). No numerical root finder or plot is used as proof. The argument only needs failure for arbitrarily large times; it proves failure for every time in the displayed half-line. It makes no assertion about smaller times or optimality of \(5\pi\).

The large-time mechanism can also be understood from \(K_R(s)\to[4\cosh^2(s/2)]^{-1}\) as \(R\uparrow\pi\): its width does not diverge. A separated two-point mixture of this limiting kernel remains bimodal. This observation motivated the construction; the finite-time rational inequalities above are the proof.

Focused primary-source searches on 6 September 2026 found the original paper, the authors' current publication pages, and Auer's 2026 paper on the law started at one. No matching later resolution of this exact compact-initial-law question was verified. This is not a certificate of current openness or originality. Author review and source checking are separate from independent review, which is pending.

Proof appendix: P0208_wreath_graph_counterexample_candidate

Candidate and author-check labels inside this appendix govern its status. Mathematical source notation is preserved.

P0208: the wreath graph and Aldous-Fill Problem 7.27

Status: complete counterexample candidate, checked by the author through hitting-time and spectral calculations. No independent review or novelty certificate. The graph itself is an established construction, not an invention here. This is a response to a historically sourced question; present-day open status remains unverified.

Primary question: Aldous and Fill, *Reversible Markov Chains and Random Walks on Graphs*, 2002 unfinished monograph, 2014 recompilation, Open Problem 7.27, PDF p.259, https://www.stat.berkeley.edu/~aldous/RWG/book.pdf . Section 7.2 is internally dated January 31, 1994. It asks, for arc-transitive graphs other than cycles on n vertices, whether maximal mean commute time is O(n log n), relaxation time is O(n), and the ratio of maximal commute time to twice stationary-average hitting time tends to one. The three assertions are counted as one related question.

Source scope and a printed definition error

Page 254 prints an inconsistent variable assignment in the definition of arc-transitivity: the images of two possible identical starting vertices would be allowed to be distinct. Read literally, that formula excludes any graph with a vertex having two neighbors. The surrounding discussion, examples of tori and Cayley graphs, and Lemma 7.19 unmistakably use the usual definition: the automorphism group is transitive on ordered adjacent pairs. Our result concerns that intended standard definition, and does not silently claim to satisfy the misprinted formula.

Conder's 2013 Rogla summer-school notes, p.8, independently describe the same established wreath/necklace graph and its arc-transitivity: https://www.famnit.upr.si/sl/resources/files/konference/rogla2013/conderlecturenotes.pdf . This is evidence that the construction is prior art. It does not establish when the probability question was answered.

Construction and symmetry

Let m be an odd integer at least five, and put

\[
 V_m=(\mathbb Z/m\mathbb Z)\times\{0,1\},\qquad
 (i,b)\sim(i+1,c),\ (i,b)\sim(i-1,c)\quad(c=0,1).
\]

This is the lexicographic product of a cycle with an edgeless two-vertex graph. It is connected, simple, 4-regular, and has n=2m vertices, so it is not an n-cycle. Dihedral symmetries act on the first coordinate. Independently swapping the two vertices in any fiber preserves every edge. Given two directed edges, a dihedral symmetry sends their ordered base edges to each other, after which independent swaps in the two distinct endpoint fibers match both second coordinates. Thus the graph is arc-transitive.

Use discrete-time simple random walk, or its rate-one continuization. The book states on p.115 that the parameters at issue are unchanged by this continuization. The first-coordinate projection is simple random walk on the m-cycle, and the second coordinate is replaced at each step by a fresh fair bit independent of the base step.

Exact hitting times

Write h(v,w) for the mean first hitting time, with h(w,w)=0. From a base position at cyclic distance j in {1,...,m-1}, the mean first arrival at base fiber zero is

\[
 H_j=j(m-j).
\]

Indeed H_0=H_m=0 and H_j=1+(H_{j-1}+H_{j+1})/2. At the first fiber arrival, the fair bit hits a specified vertex with probability one half.

Let h be the mean hitting time from its twin to the specified vertex. Starting in that fiber, the first positive return of the base walk has mean m. The arrival bit succeeds with probability one half; on failure the same twin state recurs. Hence h=m+h/2, giving h=2m. Therefore

\[
 h((j,b),(0,0))=j(m-j)+m\quad(j\ne0),\qquad
 h((0,1),(0,0))=2m.
\]

The formula is symmetric in source and target and gives 2m-1=n-1 for neighboring vertices, exactly as Lemma 7.19 requires. Its maximum is floor(m²/4)+m for m at least five. Thus

\[
 \tau^*=2\lfloor m^2/4\rfloor+2m.
\]

The stationary law is uniform. Summing the same hitting-time formula gives

\[
 \tau_0=\frac1{2m}\left[2m+2\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}
          \{j(m-j)+m\}\right]
       =\frac{m^2-1}{6}+m.
\]

On a vertex-transitive graph the stationary mean to a fixed target equals the source's stationary-average target parameter, so either equivalent convention yields this value.

Independent spectral route

For functions constant on each two-vertex fiber, the transition operator is the cycle operator, with eigenvalues cos(2πk/m), k=0,...,m-1. For functions antisymmetric within every fiber it is zero. These orthogonal invariant subspaces both have dimension m and exhaust the state space. Consequently

\[
 \tau_2=\frac1{1-\cos(2\pi/m)}\sim\frac{m^2}{2\pi^2}.
\]

The fundamental-matrix trace is

\[
 \sum_{k=1}^{m-1}\frac1{1-\cos(2\pi k/m)}+m
 =\frac{m^2-1}{6}+m,
\]

which agrees with the direct hitting-time sum. The trigonometric sum can also be recovered from the already derived cycle hitting-time formula by stationary averaging; it is not needed as an unproved numerical identity for the counterexample.

Consequences and limitations

Since n=2m, both τ* and τ2 are of order n². They violate the proposed n log n and n upper bounds. Moreover

\[
 \frac{\tau^*}{2\tau_0}
 =\frac{\lfloor m^2/4\rfloor+m}{(m^2-1)/6+m}
 \longrightarrow\frac32,
\]

which violates the third assertion. These are exact formulas, not simulation estimates.

The strongest reasons for caution are the old unfinished source, its definition typo, and the fact that wreath graphs have long been standard. Focused searches on September 6, 2026 located the original question and primary graph-theory descriptions but no verified later disposition of Problem 7.27. Absence from that search is not evidence of novelty. A future reviewer should check the intended arc-transitivity convention, rate-one normalization, and prior probability literature before any promotion.

Proof appendix: P0209_uniform_clt_counterexample_candidate

Candidate and author-check labels inside this appendix govern its status. Mathematical source notation is preserved.

P0209: a four-state obstruction to the proposed uniform normal approximation

Status: complete negative-answer candidate, author checked by spectral covariance and a direct rare-event argument. No independent review or novelty certificate. The construction responds to the literal historical question, whose current status is unverified.

Source: Aldous and Fill, *Reversible Markov Chains and Random Walks on Graphs*, 2002 unfinished monograph, 2014 recompilation, Open Problem 4.30, PDF p.137, https://www.stat.berkeley.edu/~aldous/RWG/book.pdf . The chapter is internally dated October 11, 1994. With a stationary finite reversible continuous-time chain and a function g satisfying Eπg=0 and Eπg²=1, the source asks for a universal bound

\[
 \sup_x\left|\mathbb P_\pi\left\{\frac{\int_0^t g(X_u)\,du}
 {\sigma\sqrt t}\le x\right\}-\Phi(x)\right|
 \le\psi(\|g\|_\infty,t/\tau_2),
 \qquad \psi(b,s)\longrightarrow0\quad(s\longrightarrow\infty).
\]

Here σ² is the asymptotic variance rate and τ2 is the relaxation time. We keep the first argument exactly equal to two, so there is no hidden assumption of uniformity or continuity in b.

Four-state reversible family

For an integer s at least three, set ε=s^{-2} and R=s^4. Let Y be a stationary two-state chain on {0,1}, with rates 0→1 equal to ε and 1→0 equal to 1-ε. Its stationary success probability is ε and its nonzero generator eigenvalue is -1. Independently let Z be a fair stationary sign chain, flipping at rate R/2, with nonzero generator eigenvalue -R. The product X=(Y,Z) has four states, is irreducible and reversible, and has generator eigenvalues 0,-1,-R,-(R+1). Thus τ2=1.

Define

\[
 A=2\varepsilon+\sqrt{(1-\varepsilon)(1-4\varepsilon)},\qquad
 B=\frac{2-A}{1-\varepsilon},\qquad
 g(Y,Z)=AZ+B(Y-\varepsilon).
\]

For 0<ε≤1/9, A and B are positive. The positive root A solves

\[
 A^2-4\varepsilon A+5\varepsilon-1=0,
\]

which is equivalent to A²+B²ε(1-ε)=1. Independence and centering therefore give Eπg=0 and Eπg²=1. Also A+B(1-ε)=2. Every possible absolute value of g is at most max{A+Bε,A+B(1-ε)}=2 because ε<1/2, and the value 2 occurs when Y=1,Z=1. Hence ||g||∞=2 exactly for every s. Finally A→1 and B→1.

Exact variance rate

The stationary autocovariance is

\[
 \mathbb E[g(X_0)g(X_u)]
 =A^2e^{-Ru}+B^2\varepsilon(1-\varepsilon)e^{-u}.
\]

This follows directly from the two nonconstant coordinate eigenfunctions. Integrating twice over the covariance triangle gives

\[
 \sigma^2=2\int_0^\infty\mathbb E[g(X_0)g(X_u)]\,du
 =\frac{2A^2}{R}+2B^2\varepsilon(1-\varepsilon)>0.
\]

Each fixed member of the family has the ordinary asymptotic normal limit. The issue is uniformity over chains at a time depending on the member.

Collapse at time t=s

Observe the chain until t=s. Write F=A∫₀ˢZ_u du and H=B∫₀ˢ(Y_u-ε)du. The probability that Y is ever one on this interval is at most

\[
 \mathbb P(Y_0=1)+\mathbb E[\#\{0\to1\hbox{ transitions in }[0,s]\}]
 \le\varepsilon+s\varepsilon=\frac1{s^2}+\frac1s.
\]

On the complementary event, H=-Bεs, and

\[
 \left|\frac{H}{\sigma\sqrt s}\right|
 \le\sqrt{\frac{\varepsilon s}{2(1-\varepsilon)}}\longrightarrow0.
\]

The fast component is negligible in L²:

\[
 \mathbb E F^2
 =2A^2\int_0^s(s-u)e^{-Ru}\,du
 \le\frac{2A^2s}{R},
\qquad
 \mathbb E\left(\frac F{\sigma\sqrt s}\right)^2
 \le\frac{A^2}{RB^2\varepsilon(1-\varepsilon)}\longrightarrow0.
\]

Here Rε=s². Combining the rare-event bound with Chebyshev yields

\[
 \frac{\int_0^s g(X_u)\,du}{\sigma\sqrt s}
 \longrightarrow0\quad\hbox{in probability}.
\]

For any fixed x>0 its distribution function thus tends to one. The limiting inferior of its Kolmogorov distance from a standard normal is at least 1-Φ(x). Letting x decrease to zero shows that this limiting inferior is at least one half. Yet ||g||∞=2 and t/τ2=s→∞. No function ψ of the stated kind can therefore exist.

Second checks and rate normalization

The apparent conflict with variance convergence is illusory. For a coordinate with covariance c e^{-λu}, the exact finite-time variance is 2c[t/λ-(1-e^{-λt})/λ²]. Substitution shows Var(∫g)/(σ²s)→1. Rare visits carry the variance while their probability tends to zero; the normalized squares are not uniformly integrable. Variance convergence alone cannot force a central limit theorem.

Page 26 explicitly permits nonnegative transition rates with no constraint on their sums. Even imposing total jump rate at most one would not rescue the proposed bound. Replace the generator Q by Q/R and observe for time Rs. Every outgoing rate is at most (R/2+1)/R≤1. The relaxation time becomes R, the variance rate becomes Rσ², and the time integral has the same law as R times the old integral. Both the standardized law and the ratio t/τ2=s are unchanged.

The independent coordinate construction also makes clear the missing quantity: an asymptotic variance scale relative to the function and the slow rare-event mode. A gap and a bounded instantaneous variance do not prevent the asymptotic variance from concentrating in rare excursions. This observation is explanatory, not a claim that any particular modified bound has been proved here.

Focused primary-source searches on September 6, 2026 found the original statement and the author's chapter notes, which discuss fixed-chain Berry-Esseen constants. No later resolution of this precise uniform question was verified. This is not a novelty certificate. A future reviewer should recheck the exact fixed L∞ normalization, stationary initialization, spectral gap, covariance integral, and uniform-limit quantifiers.
